\intop_{H}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f\right)\left(\omega\right)g\left(\omega\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}=\intop_{K}f\left(x\right)\mathcal{F}_{h}\left(g\right)\left(x\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}
.

(ii)

\mathcal{F}_{k}|_{K}:\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
is an injective
homeomorphism such that

\forall f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)\,\exists g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K):\ \lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}(g)=f.

(7.16)

(iii)

\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}|_{H}(\mathcal{F}_{k}|_{K}(f))=(2\pi)^{n}\left(-1\diamond f\right)

and
\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}|_{H}(\mathcal{F}_{k}^{-1}|_{K}(f))=(2\pi)^{-n}\left(-1\diamond f\right)
,
where we recall that
-1\diamond f
is the reflection of
f
.

If
H=K
, then

(iv)

(Parseval’s relation)
(2\pi)^{n}\intop_{K}f\overline{g}=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\intop_{K}\mathcal{F}_{h}\left(f\right)\overline{\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(g\right)}
.

(v)

(Plancherel’s identity)
(2\pi)^{n}\intop_{K}\left|f\right|^{2}=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\intop_{K}\left|\mathcal{F}_{h}\left(f\right)\right|^{2}
.

(vi)

\intop_{K}fg=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\intop_{K}\mathcal{F}_{h}\left(f\right)\mathcal{F}_{k}^{-1}\left(g\right)
.

In the assumptions of Cor.
64
, we can also
write all the relations involving
\lim_{h\to+\infty}
using
=_{\rho}

instead. Finally, using Thm.
35
, we can also take
the limit under the integral sign.

Proof.

(i)
follows from Def.
50

and Fubini’s theorem.

In order to prove
(ii)
, we assume
\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)=\mathcal{F}_{k}(g)
,
so that
\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)\right)=\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}(g)\right)

and hence
f=g
on
\mathring{K}
by the inversion Thm.
61
.
The equality on the entire
K
follows by sharp continuity. If
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
,
set
g:=\mathcal{F}_{k}^{-1}(f)|_{K}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
, then (
7.16
)
follows again from the Fourier inversion theorem.

To prove
(iii)
, using (
7.2
) we have
\mathcal{F}_{h}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)\right)=\mathcal{F}_{h}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}(-1\diamond(-1\diamond f))\right)=\mathcal{F}_{h}\left(-1\diamond\mathcal{F}_{k}(-1\diamond f)\right)=(2\pi)^{n}\mathcal{F}_{h}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}^{-1}(-1\diamond f)\right)\to(2\pi)^{n}(-1\diamond f)
as

h\to+\infty
.

To prove
(iv)
, use
(i)

with
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(g\right)}
instead of
g
,
then Thm.
57
.
(iii)
, and finally

(iii)
.

Plancherel’s identity
(v)
is a
trivial consequence of
(iv)
.

Finally,
(vi)
follows from
(i)
and
the inversion Thm.
61
.
∎

We close this section with a proof of the uncertainty principle:

Theorem 69
.

If
\psi\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
, then

(i)

If
\psi\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(H)\cap\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(K)
, then

\intop_{H}\omega^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}\left(\psi\right)\left(\omega\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}=\intop_{K}\omega^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}\left(\psi\right)\left(\omega\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}=:\intop\omega^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}\left(\psi\right)\left(\omega\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}

(ii)

\left(\intop x^{2}\left|\psi\left(x\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right)\left(\intop\omega^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}\left(\psi\right)\left(\omega\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{4}\|\psi\|_{2}\|\mathcal{F}(\psi)\|_{2}
.

Proof.

Properties
(ii)
and
(i)
of Thm.
39

imply that also
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(H)
. Therefore, Plancherel’s identity
Thm.
68
.
(v)

yields

\int_{H}\left|\psi^{\prime}\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{H}\left|\mathcal{F}(\psi^{\prime})\right|^{2}.

But
\left|\mathcal{F}(\psi^{\prime})\right|^{2}=\omega^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}(\psi)\right|^{2}

from Thm.
57
.
(viii)
because
\psi

is compactly supported

and hence
\Delta_{1k}\psi=0
. Therefore

\int_{H}\left|\psi^{\prime}\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{H}\omega^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}(\psi)(\omega)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}.

(7.17)

At the same result we arrive considering
K
instead of
H
. Finally,
we apply Def.
40
of integral of a compactly supported
GSF, which yields independence from the functionally compact integration
domain.

To prove inequality
(ii)
, we assume that
\psi\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(K)
;
using integration by parts, we get:

\displaystyle\int x\overline{\psi(x)}\psi^{\prime}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-k}^{k}x\overline{\psi(x)}\psi^{\prime}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\left[x\overline{\psi(x)}\psi(x)\right]_{x=-k}^{x=k}-\int\psi(x)\left(\overline{\psi(x)}+x\overline{\psi^{\prime}(x)}\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=-\int\left[\left|\psi(x)\right|^{2}+x\psi(x)\overline{\psi^{\prime}(x)}\right]\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}.

Thereby

\displaystyle\int\left|\psi(x)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}

\displaystyle=-2\text{Re}\left(\int x\psi(x)\overline{\psi^{\prime}(x)}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\leq 2\left|\text{Re}\left(\int x\psi(x)\overline{\psi^{\prime}(x)}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right)\right|\leq

\displaystyle\leq 2\int\left|x\psi(x)\overline{\psi^{\prime}(x)}\right|\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}.

Where we used the triangle inequality for integrals (see Thm.
33
.
(iv)
).
Using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (see Thm.
45
), we
hence obtain

\displaystyle\left(\int\left|\psi(x)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\int\left|x\psi(x)\overline{\psi^{\prime}(x)}\right|\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right)^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\int x^{2}\left|\psi(x)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right)\left(\int\left|\psi^{\prime}(x)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right).

From this, thanks to (
7.17
) and Plancherel’s equality,
the claim follows.
∎

Note that if
\|\psi\|_{2}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
is invertible,
then also
\|\mathcal{F}(\psi)\|_{2}
is invertible by Plancherel’s
equality, and we can hence write the uncertainty principle in the
usual normalized form.

Example 70
.

On the contrary with respect the classical
formulation in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
of the uncertainty principle, in Thm.
69

we can e.g. consider
\psi=\delta\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
, and we have

\int x^{2}\delta(x)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=\left[\int_{-1}^{1}x^{2}b_{\varepsilon}^{2}\psi_{\varepsilon}(b_{\varepsilon}x)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right]

where
\psi(x)=[\psi_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]
is a Colombeau mollifier
and
b=[b_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
is a strong infinite number (see Example

24
). Since normalizing the function
\varepsilon\mapsto b_{\varepsilon}^{2}\psi_{\varepsilon}(b_{\varepsilon}x)^{2}

we get an approximate identity, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{-1}^{1}x^{2}b_{\varepsilon}^{2}\psi_{\varepsilon}(b_{\varepsilon}x)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=0
,
and hence
\int x^{2}\delta(x)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\approx 0
is an infinitesimal.
The uncertainty principle Thm.
69
implies that
it is an invertible infinitesimal. Considering the HFT
\mathbb{1}=\mathcal{F}(\delta)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
,
we have

\int\omega^{2}\mathbb{1}(\omega)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}\geq\int_{-r}^{r}\omega^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}=2\frac{r^{3}}{3}\quad\forall r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

Thereby,
\int\omega^{2}\mathbb{1}(\omega)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}
is
an infinite number.

8.
Preservation of classical Fourier transform

It is natural to inquire the relations between classical FT of tempered
distributions and our HFT.

Let us start with a couple of exploring examples:

(i)

\mathcal{F}_{k}(1)(\omega)=\int_{-k}^{k}1\cdot e^{-ix\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=\int_{-k}^{k}\cos(x\omega)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}
.
If
L\subseteq_{0}I
and
\omega|_{L}
is invertible (see Sec.
2.3

for the language of subpoints), then
\mathcal{F}_{k}(1)(\omega)=_{L}2\frac{\sin(k\omega)}{\omega}
;
if
\omega=_{L}0
, then
\mathcal{F}_{k}(1)(\omega)=2k
. Classically,
we have
\hat{1}=2\pi\delta
.

(ii)

\mathcal{F}_{k}(H)(\omega)=\int_{-k}^{k}H(x)e^{-ix\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}
.
Assuming that
\omega|_{L}
is invertible on
L\subseteq_{0}I
, we have

\mathcal{F}_{k}(H)(\omega)=_{L}\frac{i}{\omega}e^{-ik\omega}-\frac{i}{\omega}\mathbb{1}(\omega)
.
Classically, we have
\hat{H}=\pi\delta-\frac{i}{\omega}
. Therefore,
if
k
is a strong infinite number and
\omega
is far from the
origin,
|\omega|\geq r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have
\mathcal{F}_{k}(H)(\omega)=\iota_{\mathbb{R}}^{b}\left(\hat{H}\right)(\omega)

(here
\iota_{\mathbb{R}}^{b}
is an embedding of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
into

\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\mathbb{R}}))
, see Sec.
8
). However, the
latter equality does not hold if
\omega\approx 0
.

Since classically we do not have infinite numbers, the former of these
examples leads us to the following idea

\mathcal{F}(1\cdot\mathbb{1})=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{F}(\delta))=2\pi\left(-1\diamond\delta\right)=2\pi\delta.

Note that if
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
, then
\left(f\cdot\mathbb{1}\right)(\omega)=f(\omega)

for all finite point
\omega\in K
. We therefore call
f\cdot\mathbb{1}

the
finite part of
f
. The same idea works for
e^{iax}

and hence also for
\sin
,
\cos
. Let us now consider
\delta\cdot\mathbb{1}
:

\mathcal{F}(\delta\cdot\mathbb{1})(\omega)=\int\delta(x)\mathcal{F}(\delta)(x)e^{-ix\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}.

We recall that integrating against
\delta
yields the evaluation
of the second factor at
0
only if the latter is bounded by a tame
polynomial at
0
(see Example
48
.
(iv)
).
But the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\delta)(x)e^{-ix\omega}
is
bounded by a tame polynomial at
x=0
for all
\omega
, and we get

\mathcal{F}(\delta\cdot\mathbb{1})(\omega)=1
. Being bounded by
a tame polynomial is, in general, a necessary assumption because

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(H\cdot\mathbb{1})(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int H(x)\cdot\mathcal{F}(\delta)(x)e^{-ix\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\int\delta(x)\mathcal{F}_{k}(H\cdot e^{-i(-)\omega})(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\int\delta(x)\mathcal{F}_{k}(H)(x+\omega)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}},

but
\mathcal{F}_{k}(H)(x+\omega)=\frac{i}{x+\omega}e^{-ik(x+\omega)}-\frac{i}{x+\omega}\mathbb{1}(x+\omega)

is not bounded by a tame polynomial at
x=0
if
\omega\approx 0

because of the terms
\frac{1}{\omega}
.

These exploratory examples lead us to the following

Theorem 71
.

Let
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
, and assume
that
\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)
is bounded by a tame polynomial at
\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Then
\mathcal{F}(f\cdot\mathbb{1})(\omega)=\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)
.

Proof.

It suffices to apply Thm.
68
.
(i)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(f\cdot\mathbb{1})(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int f(x)\mathcal{F}(\delta)(x)e^{-ix\cdot\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\int\delta(x)\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f\cdot e^{-i(-)\cdot\omega}\right)(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\int\delta(x)\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f\right)(x+\omega)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f\right)(\omega).

∎

Since
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)\right](\omega)=-i\mathcal{F}_{k}(x_{j}f)(\omega)
,
we have the following sufficient condition for
\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)

being bounded by a tame polynomial at
\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
:

Theorem 72
.

Let
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
be a large infinite
number, and let
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
be uniformly bounded by a tame polynomial
at
K
, i.e.

\exists M,c\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\,\forall y\in K\,\forall j\in\mathbb{N}:\ \left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}}{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}$}}f(y)\right|\leq M\cdot c^{j},\quad\frac{b}{c}\text{ is a large infinite number}.

(8.1)

Then for all
\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the HFT
\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)
is
bounded by a tame polynomial at
\omega
. In particular, every
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

satisfies condition (
8.1
), and hence

\mathcal{F}(f)=\mathcal{F}(f\cdot\mathbb{1})=\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(\hat{f}),

(8.2)

where
\hat{f}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the classical FT of
f
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}}{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}$}}\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{h\leq j}\left|\mathcal{F}_{k}(x_{h}f)(\omega)\right|\leq\max_{h\leq j}\int_{K}\left|x_{h}f(x)\right|\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\leq

\displaystyle\leq Mc^{j}\max_{h\leq j}\int_{K}|x_{h}|\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=:\bar{M}c^{j}.

If
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}}{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}$}}f(y)\right|\in\mathbb{R}
,
so that if
b\geq\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-r}
,
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, it suffices to
take
c=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-r+s}
where
0<s<r
to have that (
8.1
)
holds. The last equality in (
8.2
) is equivalent
to say that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(x)e^{-ix\cdot\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=\int_{K}f(x)e^{-ix\cdot\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}
,
which can be proved as for the Gaussian, see Lem.
56
.
∎

We can now consider the relations between
\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(\hat{T})

and
\mathcal{F}_{k}(\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T))
when
T\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
A first trivial link is given by the so-called
equality in the
sense of generalized tempered distributions
: For all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
from (
3.4
) we have

\int\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(\hat{T})\varphi=\langle\hat{T},\varphi\rangle=\langle T,\hat{\varphi}\rangle=\int\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T)\hat{\varphi}.

Using the previous Thm.
72
we get
\hat{\varphi}=\mathcal{F}(\varphi)

(identifying a smooth function with its embedding). Thereby

\int\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(\hat{T})\varphi=\int\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T)\mathcal{F}(\varphi)=\int\mathcal{F}\left(\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T)\right)\varphi\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(8.3)

In Colombeau’s theory, this relation is usually written
\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(\hat{T})=_{\text{g.t.d.}}\mathcal{F}\left(\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T)\right)
.

In the following result, we give a sufficient condition to have a
pointwise equality between the HFT of the finite part of
\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T)

and
\hat{T}
:

Theorem 73
.

Let
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
be a large infinite
number and
T\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Assume that
\mathcal{F}(\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T))

is bounded by a tame polynomial at
\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then

\mathcal{F}_{k}(\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T))(\omega)=\mathcal{F}(\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(T)\cdot\mathbb{1})(\omega)=\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(\hat{T})(\omega).

Proof.

For simplicity of notation, we use
\iota:=\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}
. Using
Thm.
71
, we have

\mathcal{F}\left(\iota(T)\cdot\mathbb{1}\right)(\omega)=\mathcal{F}_{k}(\iota(T))(\omega).

Let
\psi(x)=[\psi_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]

be an
n
-
dimensional
Colombeau mollifier defined by
b
, and set
K_{\varepsilon}:=[-k_{\varepsilon},k_{\varepsilon}]^{n}
;
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\iota(T)\right)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\left[\int_{K_{\varepsilon}}\langle T(y),\psi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\rangle e^{-ix\cdot\omega_{\varepsilon}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right]=

\displaystyle=\left[\langle T(y),\int\psi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)e^{-ix\cdot\omega_{\varepsilon}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\rangle\right]=

\displaystyle=\left[\langle T(y),\widehat{y\oplus\psi_{\varepsilon}}(\omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle\right]=

\displaystyle=\left[\langle\hat{T}(y),\left(y\oplus\psi_{\varepsilon}\right)(\omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle\right]=

\displaystyle=\left[\langle\hat{T}(y),\psi_{\varepsilon}(\omega_{\varepsilon}-y)\rangle\right]=\iota(\hat{T})(\omega).

∎

8.1.
Fourier transform in the Colombeau’s setting

Only in this section we assume a very basic knowledge of Colombeau’s
theory.

Assume that
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open set. The algebra

\mathcal{G}_{\tau}^{s}\left(\Omega\right)
of tempered generalized
functions was introduced by J.F. Colombeau in
[
10
]
, in order
to develop a theory of Fourier transform. Since then, there has been
a rapid development of Fourier analysis, regularity theory and microlocal
analysis in this setting.

Definition 74
.

The
\mathcal{G}_{\tau}^{s}(\Omega)
algebra of
Colombeau tempered
GF
(trivially generalized by using an arbitrary gauge
\rho
) is
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\tau}^{s}\left(\Omega\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left(u_{\varepsilon}\right)\in\mbox{$\mathcal{C}$}^{\infty}(\Omega)^{I}\mid\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\,\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{:=\qquad}\left.\sup_{x\in\Omega}\left(1+\left|x\right|\right)^{-N}\left|\partial^{\alpha}u_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)\right|=O(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-N})\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\tau}^{s}\left(\Omega\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left(u_{\varepsilon}\right)\in\mbox{$\mathcal{C}$}^{\infty}(\Omega)^{I}\mid\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\,\exists p\in\mathbb{N}\,\forall m\in\mathbb{N}:\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{:=\qquad}\left.\sup_{x\in\Omega}\left(1+\left|x\right|\right)^{-p}\left|\partial^{\alpha}u_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)\right|=O(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{m})\right\},

\mathcal{G}_{\tau}^{s}(\Omega):=\mathcal{E}_{\tau}^{s}(\Omega)/\mathcal{N}_{\tau}^{s}(\Omega).

Colombeau tempered GF can be embedded as GSF, at least if
the internal set
[\Omega]
is sharply bounded. We first define

Definition 75
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then

\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\tau}(X):=\left\{u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(X)\mid\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\,\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\,\forall x\in X:\ \left|\partial^{\alpha}u\left(x\right)\right|\leq\frac{\left(1+\left|x\right|\right)^{N}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{N}}\right\}.

Theorem 76
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set such
that
[\Omega]
is sharply bounded. A Colombeau tempered GF
u=(u_{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{N}^{s}_{\tau}(\Omega)\in\mathcal{G}^{s}_{\tau}(\Omega)

defines a GSF
u:[x_{\varepsilon}]\in[\Omega]\longrightarrow[u_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}
.
This assignment provides an algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{G}^{s}_{\tau}(\Omega)\simeq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\tau}([\Omega])
.

Integration of a CGF
u=[u_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathcal{G}^{s}(\Omega)
over a standard

K\Subset\Omega
can be defined
\varepsilon
-wise as
\int_{K}u\left(x\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}:=\left[\int_{K}u_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Similarly we can proceed for
\int_{\Omega}u
if
u
is compactly
supported and
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an arbitrary open set. On
the other hand, to define the Fourier transform, we have to integrate
tempered CGF on the entire
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Using this integration of CGF,
this is accomplished by multiplying the generalized function by a
so-called
damping measure

\varphi
, see e.g.
[
35
]
:

Definition 77
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi=1
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}x^{\alpha}\varphi(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=0
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
,
and set
\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x):=\rho_{\varepsilon}\odot\varphi(x)=\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-n}\varphi(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-1}x)
.
Let
u=[u_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathcal{G}_{\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
u_{\hat{\varphi}}:=[u_{\varepsilon}\widehat{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}]
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}_{\hat{\varphi}}x:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u_{\hat{\varphi}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u_{\varepsilon}(x)\widehat{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right]\in\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}
,
where
\widehat{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}
denotes the classical FT. In particular,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}(u)(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-ix\omega}u(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}_{\hat{\varphi}}x=\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-ix\omega}u_{\varepsilon}(x)\widehat{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right]

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{*}(u)(x)

\displaystyle:=(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-ix\omega}u(\omega)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}_{\hat{\varphi}}\omega=\left[(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-ix\omega}u_{\varepsilon}(\omega)\widehat{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}(\omega)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}\right].

As a result, although this notion of Fourier transform in the Colombeau
setting shares several properties with the classical one, it lacks
e.g. the Fourier inversion theorem, which holds only at the level
of equality in the sense of generalized tempered distributions
[
11
,
13
,
48
]
,
see also (
8.3
). See also
[
60
]
for a Paley-Wiener
like theorem. In other words, we only have e.g.
\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}(\partial^{\alpha}u)=_{\text{g.t.d.}}i^{|\alpha|}\omega^{\alpha}\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}(u)
,

i^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{F^{*}}_{\hat{\varphi}}(\partial^{\alpha}u)=_{\text{g.t.d.}}x^{\alpha}\mathcal{F^{*}}_{\hat{\varphi}}(u)
,

\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{F^{*}}_{\hat{\varphi}}u=_{\text{g.t.d.}}\mathcal{F^{*}}_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}u
,
where
\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}(u)
denotes the Fourier transform
with respect to the damping measure. Moreover
\langle\iota_{\mathbb{R}}(\hat{T}),\psi\rangle\approx\langle\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}\iota_{\mathbb{R}}(T),\psi\rangle

for all
T\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and all
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
where
\iota_{\mathbb{R}}(T)
is the embedding of Thm.
22
.
Intuitively, one could say that the use of the multiplicative damping
measure introduces a perturbation of infinitesimal frequencies that
inhibit several results that, on the contrary, hold for the HFT. On
the other hand, HFT lies on a better integration theory that allows
to integrate any GSF on the functionally compact set
K
. The only
possibilities to obtain a strict Fourier inversion theorem in Colombeau’s
theory, are the approach used by
[
49
]
, where smoothing kernels
are used as index set (instead of the simpler
\varepsilon\in I
) and therefore
the knowledge of infinite dimensional calculus in convenient vector
spaces is needed, or
[
54
,
7
]
, which are based on the
Colombeau space
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}))
, but where the imbedding
of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
is more complicated.

Finally, the following result links the HFT with the FT of tempered
CGF as defined above.

Theorem 78
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set
such that
[\Omega]
is sharply bounded, and let
u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\tau}([\Omega])

be a tempered CGF (identified with the corresponding GSF). Finally,
let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a dumping measure. Then

\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}(u)=\mathcal{F}\left[u\cdot\hat{\varphi}((-)\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}})\right]=\mathcal{F}\left[u\cdot\mathcal{F}(\varphi)((-)\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}})\right].

Proof.

Def. (
77
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hat{\varphi}}(u)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u(x)e^{-ix\cdot\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}_{\hat{\varphi}}x}{\hbox{\rm d}_{\hat{\varphi}}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}_{\hat{\varphi}}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}_{\hat{\varphi}}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u(x)e^{-ix\cdot\omega}\widehat{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\odot\varphi}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u(x)e^{-ix\cdot\omega}\left(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\diamond\hat{\varphi}\right)(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u(x)e^{-ix\cdot\omega}\hat{\varphi}(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\cdot x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}\left[u\cdot\hat{\varphi}((-)\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}})\right](\omega)=

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}\left[u\cdot\mathcal{F}(\varphi)((-)\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}})\right](\omega),

where, in the last equality, we applied (
8.2
).
∎

9.
Examples and applications

In this section we present an initial study of possible applications
of the HFT. Our main aim is to highlight the new potentialities of
the theory. For example, thanks to the possibility of applying the
HFT also to non-tempered GF, the next deductions are fully rigorous,
even if they correspond to the frequently used statement: “proceeding
formally, we obtain…”. We also note that the FT is often used
to prove necessary conditions: if the solution
y
satisfies a given
differential equation, then necessarily
y=\ldots
In the following,
we propose an attempt to also reverse this implication, even if this
depends on a suitable
extensibility property
: If the HFT of
the given differential equation holds on some space, e.g.
\mathring{K}\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
,
then it also holds on the entire
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.
We discuss some sufficient conditions for this property to hold, but
a thorough study of this condition is out of the scope of the present
work.

9.1.
Applications of HFT to ordinary differential equations

The simplest ODE

We first consider the following, apparently trivial but actually meaningful,
example:

y^{\prime}=y,\ y\left(0\right)=c,\ y\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}\left(\left[-k,k\right]\right),\ c\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},

(9.1)

where
k=-\log\left(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\right)
. Since we do not impose limitations
on the initial value
c
, this simple example clearly shows the possibilities
of the HFT to manage non tempered generalized functions. Applying
the HFT to both sides of (
9.1
) and using the
derivation formula (
6.1
), we get

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(y\right)=\Delta_{1k}y+i\omega\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(y\right).

(9.2)

Set for simplicity
\Delta_{y}\left(\omega\right):=\Delta_{1k}y\left(\omega\right)=y(k)e^{-ik\omega}-y(-k)e^{ik\omega}

and note that the function
\Delta_{y}
does not depend on the whole
function
y
but only on the two values
y(\pm k)
. We get
\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(y\right)\left(\omega\right)=\frac{\Delta_{y}\left(\omega\right)}{1-i\omega}
,
and applying the Fourier inversion Thm.
61
, we obtain

y(x)=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{y}\left(\omega\right)}{1-i\omega}\right)(x)\quad\forall x\in\mathring{K}.

(9.3)

Using the initial condition in (
9.1
), we have

y\left(0\right)=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{y}\left(\omega\right)}{1-i\omega}\right)\left(0\right)=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\int_{-h}^{h}\frac{\Delta_{y}\left(\omega\right)}{1-i\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}=c.

(9.4)

Clearly, e.g. by separation of variables, (
9.1
)
necessarily yields
y(x)=ce^{x}
for all
x\in[-k,k]
. Therefore,

y\left(k\right)=ce^{-\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}}=\frac{c}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}}
,
y\left(-k\right)=ce^{\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}}=c\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}

and
\Delta_{y}\left(\omega\right)=c\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{i\omega-1}}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{i\omega-1}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{i\omega-1}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{i\omega-1}$}}-c\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{-i\omega+1}}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{-i\omega+1}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{-i\omega+1}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{-i\omega+1}$}}

because
\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{i\omega}}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{i\omega}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{i\omega}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho^{i\omega}$}}=e^{-ik\omega}
.

Vice versa, take
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, and set

\Delta_{a,b}^{k}(\omega):=a\cdot e^{-ik\omega}-b\cdot e^{ik\omega}\quad\forall\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}.

We also assume the following compatibility conditions on
a
,
b
:

\begin{cases}a=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{a,b}^{k}(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\right)(k)\\
b=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{a,b}^{k}(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\right)(-k)\end{cases}

(9.5)

We will see in Rem.
79
.
(i)
that actually
these conditions overdetermine
a
,
b
. Set

y(x):=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{a,b}^{k}(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\right)(x)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\quad\forall x\in K,

(9.6)

where we also assumed that the limit in (
9.6
) exists.
Thereby, (
9.6
) and (
9.5
) imply
\Delta_{a,b}^{k}(\omega)=\Delta_{1k}y(\omega)
.
Now, apply
\mathcal{F}_{k}
to both sides of (
9.6
)
and use Thm.
35
, Thm.
61
to get

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}(y)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{1k}y(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\right)\right)(\omega)

(9.7)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{1k}y(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\right)\right)(\omega)

(9.8)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\Delta_{1k}y(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\right)\right)(\omega)

(9.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{1k}y(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\quad\forall\omega\in\mathring{K}.

(9.10)

Note that in (
9.9
) we used Fubini’s theorem to exchange

\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}
with
\mathcal{F}_{k}
. We can now reverse
all the calculations leading us to the necessary condition (
9.3
)
to obtain

\mathcal{F}_{k}(y)(\omega)=\mathcal{F}_{k}(y^{\prime})(\omega)\quad\forall\omega\in\mathring{K}.

(9.11)

We would like to apply
\mathcal{F}_{h}
to both sides of (
9.11
)
and then take
\lim_{h\to+\infty}
. However, to make this step, we
need equality (
9.11
) to hold for all
\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

because
h\to+\infty
,
h\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. We therefore assume that (
9.11
)
can be extended from
\mathring{K}
to the entire
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
(note that
both sides of (
9.11
) are GSF defined on
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
):

\mathcal{F}_{k}(y)|_{\mathring{K}}=\mathcal{F}_{k}(y^{\prime})|_{\mathring{K}}\Rightarrow\ \mathcal{F}_{k}(y)=\mathcal{F}_{k}(y^{\prime})\text{ on }\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.12)

This is the aforementioned
extensibility property for the ODE

y^{\prime}=y
. Under this assumption, we can apply the Fourier inversion
Thm.
61
to obtain
y(x)=y^{\prime}(x)
for all
x\in\mathring{K}
,
and hence
y=y^{\prime}
by continuity. We can simply, and more generally,
state the extensibility property saying:
if the HFT of the differential
equation holds in
\mathring{K}
for the function
y
, then it holds
everywhere
.

Remark 79
.

(i)

Since
y(x)=y(0)e^{x}
, compatibility conditions
(
9.5
) imply
a=y(0)e^{k}
and
b=y(0)e^{-k}
.
If
y(0)
is invertible, we obtain
a=be^{2k}
. Therefore, (
9.5
)
overdetermine the constants
a
,
b
.

(ii)

Using the notion of hyperseries we already mentioned in Sec.
4.1

(see
[
62
]
), we can say that if both
\mathcal{F}_{k}(y)

and
\mathcal{F}_{k}(y^{\prime})
can be expanded in Taylor hyperseries (we
can say that they are
real hyper analytic
), i.e. if for some

\bar{\omega}\in\mathring{K}
and for all
\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}(y)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho\hskip-5.49028pt$}$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho\hskip-5.49028pt$}$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho\hskip-5.49028pt$}$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho\hskip-5.49028pt$}$}}}\sum_{n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\,}}{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}\sum_{n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\,}}{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}\sum_{n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\,}}{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}\sum_{n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\,}}\frac{\mathcal{F}_{k}(y)^{(n)}(\bar{\omega})}{n!}\cdot(\omega-\bar{\omega})^{n}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}(y^{\prime})(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho\hskip-5.49028pt$}$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho\hskip-5.49028pt$}$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho\hskip-5.49028pt$}$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho\hskip-5.49028pt$}$}}}\sum_{n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\,}}{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}\sum_{n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\,}}{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}\sum_{n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\,}}{\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}{{\vphantom{\sum}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho\hskip-2.04861pt$}}}\sum_{n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\,}}\frac{\mathcal{F}_{k}(y^{\prime})^{(n)}(\bar{\omega})}{n!}\cdot(\omega-\bar{\omega})^{n},

then (
9.12
) holds, because (
9.11
) yields

\mathcal{F}_{k}(y)^{(n)}(\bar{\omega})=\mathcal{F}_{k}(y^{\prime})^{(n)}(\bar{\omega})

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Even if this ODE is the simplest one, we want to underline our deductions
with the following statements:

Theorem 80
.

If
k=-\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}
,
y\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
,
\Delta_{y}:=\Delta_{1k}y

and
y^{\prime}=y
, then

y(x)=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{y}\left(\omega\right)}{1-i\omega}\right)(x)\quad\forall x\in\mathring{K}.

The sufficient condition deduction corresponds to the following

Theorem 81
.

Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, and set

\displaystyle\Delta_{a,b}^{k}(\omega):=a\cdot e^{-ik\omega}-b\cdot e^{ik\omega}\quad\forall\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\forall x\in K\,\exists\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{a,b}^{k}(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\right)(x)=:y(x)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}.

Assume the compatibility conditions (
9.5
) and the
extensibility property for the ODE
y^{\prime}=y
, i.e. (
9.12
).
Then

\begin{cases}y^{\prime}=y\text{ on }K\\
y(0)=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\int_{-h}^{h}\frac{\Delta_{a,b}^{k}(\omega)}{1-i\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}.\end{cases}

We finally underscore that:

(a)

In the classical theory, the lacking of the term
\Delta_{1k}y(\omega)

does not allow to obtain the non-tempered solution for
c\neq 0
: in
other words, if
\Delta_{1k}y=0
, then (
9.4
) implies that
necessarily
c=0
.

(b)

In the previous deduction, it is clearly important that the HFT can
be applied to all the GF of the space
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
.

(c)

Compare (
9.3
) with Example
55
to note that
if
c\geq r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then in (
9.3
) we are considering
the inverse HFT of a GSF which always takes infinite values for all
finite
\omega
. Clearly, this strongly motivates the use of a non-Archimedean
framework for this type of problems.

(d)

All our results, in particular the inversion Thm.
61
,
hold for an arbitrary infinite number
k
. In this particular case,
we considered
k
of logarithmic type to get moderateness of the
exponential function.

General constant coefficient ODE

Let us consider an arbitrary
n
-th order constant (generalized)
coefficient ODE

a_{n}y^{\left(n\right)}+\ldots a_{1}y^{\left(1\right)}+a_{0}y=g,\quad y,g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right]),\ a_{j}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

(9.13)

Note that simply assuming to have a solution
y
defined on the infinite
interval
[-k,k]
, already yields an implicit limitation on the coefficients

a_{j}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. In fact, the equation
y^{\prime}-\frac{1}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}}y=0

has solution
y(x)=y(0)e^{x/\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}}
, which is defined only
if
x\leq-N\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\approx 0
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thereby, its domain will never be of the type
[-k,k]
unless
y(0)=0
.
By applying the HFT to both sides of equation (
9.13
),
the differential equation is converted into the algebraic equation

P\cdot\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(y\right)+\Delta_{y}=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(g\right),

(9.14)

where

P\left(\omega\right):=\sum_{j=0}^{n}a_{j}\left(i\omega\right)^{j},

and
\Delta_{y}\left(\omega\right)
is the sum of all the extra terms
in Thm.
57
.
(viii)
, which in this
case becomes

\Delta_{y}(\omega):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\cdot\sum_{p=1}^{j}(i\omega)^{j-p}\Delta_{1k}y^{(p-1)}(\omega)\quad\forall\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}.

Note that the function
\Delta_{y}
depends on the points
y^{(p)}(\pm k)

for
p=0,\ldots,n-1
. Assuming that
P(\omega)
is invertible for
all
\omega\in K
, from (
9.14
) and
the inversion Thm.
61
, we get

y(x)=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}_{k}(g)-\Delta_{y}}{P}\right)(x)\quad\forall x\in\mathring{K}.

(9.15)

Proceeding as in the previous example, i.e. using again the inversion
Thm.
61
, the differentiation formula (
6.1
)
and assuming suitable compatibility and extensibility conditions,
we can actually prove that (
9.15
) yields a solution
of (
9.13
). For a generalization to GSF of the usual
results about
n
-th order constant generalized coefficient ODE,
see
[
45
]
.

Airy equation

A simple example of non-constant coefficient linear ODE is given by
the Airy equation

\displaystyle u^{\prime\prime}(x)-x\cdot u(x)=0,\quad u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right],\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}).

(9.16)

By applying the derivative formulas Thm.
57
.
(viii)

and Thm.
57
.
(ix)
, we get

i\omega^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)+\omega\Delta_{1k}u-i\Delta_{1k}u^{\prime}-\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k}\left(u\right)=0.

Let us now set
\Delta_{u}\left(\omega\right):=\omega\Delta_{1k}u\left(\omega\right)-i\Delta_{1k}u^{\prime}\left(\omega\right)
,

\forall\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. Once again, the function
\Delta_{u}
depends
on the points
u\left(\pm k\right)
and
u^{\prime}\left(\pm k\right)
.

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k}\left(u\right)-i\omega^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)=\Delta_{u}.

(9.17)

Equation (
9.17
) is a first order non-constant coefficient,
non-homogeneous generalized ODE with respect to the variable
\omega
.
We can solve it e.g. by considering the integrating factor
\mu\left(\omega\right):=e^{\int_{0}^{\omega}-iz^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}}=e^{-i\frac{\omega^{3}}{3}}
.
Then, the solution of (
9.17
) is given by

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)\left(\omega\right)=\frac{\intop_{0}^{\omega}\mu\left(z\right)\Delta_{u}\left(z\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}+c}{\mu\left(\omega\right)}=\frac{\intop_{0}^{\omega}e^{-\frac{iz^{3}}{3}}\Delta_{u}\left(z\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}+c}{e^{-\frac{i\omega^{3}}{3}}}\quad\forall\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},

where
c:=\mathcal{F}_{k}(u)(0)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. Finally, we apply the
inversion Thm.
61
and substitute
\Delta_{u}\left(\omega\right)

to recover the original function

\displaystyle u(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\intop_{0}^{\omega}e^{-\frac{iz^{3}}{3}}\Delta_{u}\left(z\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}+c}{e^{-\frac{i\omega^{3}}{3}}}\right)(x)=

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\intop_{0}^{\omega}e^{-\frac{iz^{3}}{3}}\Delta_{u}\left(z\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}}{e^{-\frac{i\omega^{3}}{3}}}\right)(x)+\lim_{h\to+\infty}\frac{c}{\pi}\int_{0}^{h}\cos\left(\frac{\omega^{3}}{3}+\omega x\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{h}\cos\left(\omega x+\frac{\omega^{3}}{3}\right)\int_{0}^{\omega}e^{-i\left(hz+\frac{z^{3}}{3}\right)}\left(zu\left(k\right)-iu^{\prime}\left(k\right)\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\lim_{h\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{h}\cos\left(\omega x+\frac{\omega^{3}}{3}\right)\int_{0}^{\omega}e^{-i\left(-hz+\frac{z^{3}}{3}\right)}\left(zu\left(-k\right)-iu^{\prime}\left(-k\right)\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\hbox{\rm d}z}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}z$}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\lim_{h\to+\infty}\frac{c}{\pi}\int_{0}^{h}\cos\left(\frac{\omega^{3}}{3}+\omega x\right)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}\quad\forall x\in K.

If we assume that
u(\pm k)\approx 0
and
u^{\prime}\left(\pm k\right)\approx 0
,
then we get the first Airy function up to infinitesimals
u(x)\approx c\cdot\text{Ai}(x)
.
Therefore, if
u(\pm k)\not\approx 0
or
u^{\prime}\left(\pm k\right)\not\approx 0

and
c=0
, we must get, up to infinitesimals, a multiple of the second
Airy function (see e.g.
[
1
]
)

\exists d\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}:\ u(x)\approx\text{Bi}(x)=\frac{d}{\pi}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left\{\exp\left(-\frac{t^{3}}{3}+xt\right)+\sin\left(\frac{t^{3}}{3}+xt\right)\right\}\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}.

We explicitly recall that
\text{Bi}(x)
is of exponential order
as
x\to+\infty
and hence it is not a tempered distribution, so
that classically we miss this solution.

9.2.
Applications of HFT to partial differential equations

The wave equation

Let us consider the one dimensional (generalized) wave equation

\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial t^{2}}=c^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}},\quad c\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},\ u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right]\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}),

(9.18)

where
c
is invertible, and subject to the initial conditions at

t=0

\displaystyle u(-,0)

\displaystyle=f,\quad\partial_{t}u(-,0)=g,

(9.19)

Where
f
,
g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
. As usual, we directly apply
the HFT
\mathcal{F}_{k}
with respect to the variable
x
to both
sides and then apply the derivation formula Thm.
57
.
(viii)

to the right hand side

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial t^{2}}\right)=c^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}}\right),

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)}{\partial t^{2}}=-c^{2}\omega^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)+i\omega\Delta_{1k}u+\Delta_{1k}\left(\partial_{x}u\right).

Note that also the
\Delta_{1k}
-terms refer to the variable
x
,
but the result is a function of
t
. More precisely, set

\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right):=i\omega\Delta_{1k}\left(u(-,t)\right)(\omega)+\Delta_{1k}\left(\partial_{x}u(-,t)\right)(\omega).

(9.20)

The function
\Delta_{u}
does not depend on the whole functions

u
and
\partial_{x}u
but only on its boundary values:
u\left(\pm k,-\right)

and
\partial_{x}u\left(\pm k,-\right)
, which are functions of
t
.
Hence, we get

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)}{\partial t^{2}}(\omega,-)+c^{2}\omega^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)(\omega,-)=\Delta_{u}(\omega,-)\quad\forall\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.21)

We obtain, for each fixed
\omega
, a constant (generalized) coefficient,

non
-homogeneous, second order ODE in the unknown

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)\left(\omega,-\right)
. Clearly, (
9.21
)
already highlights a difference with the classical FT, where
\Delta_{u}=0
.
To solve equation (
9.21
), we can use the standard method
of variation of parameters to get

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}(u)(\omega,t)

\displaystyle=d_{2}(\omega)tS(c\omega t)+d_{1}(\omega)\cos(c\omega t)+

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+tS(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)\cos(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}-

(9.22)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\cos(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}s\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)S(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}},

(9.23)

\displaystyle S(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\cos(zt)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}.

(9.24)

More precisely, in the previous step we applied the general theory
of linear constant generalized coefficient, non-homogeneous ODE developed
in
[
45
]
, which generalizes the classical theory proving
that the space of all the solutions is a
2
-dimensional
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
-module,
generated in this case by
tS(c\omega t)
and
\cos(c\omega t)
,
and translated by a particular solution of (
9.21
). Explicitly
note that every functions in (
9.23
) is a smooth function
or a GSF. We also note that the functions
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
satisfy

\displaystyle d_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)-\int_{0}^{1}s\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)S(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}

\displaystyle d_{2}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{k}(g)(\omega)+\int_{0}^{1}\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)\cos(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}.

They hence depend on the functions
f
,
g
of the initial conditions
(
9.19
), but also on the unknown function
u
because
of (
9.20
). Finally, applying the inversion Thm.
61
,
for all the interior points
x\in\mathring{K}
and all
t\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
,
we get

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\left\{\phantom{\int_{1}^{t}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(d_{2}(\omega)tS(c\omega t)+d_{1}(\omega)\cos(c\omega t)\right)(x,t)+\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(tS(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)\cos(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right)(x,t)-

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\left.\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\cos(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}s\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)S(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right)(x,t)\right\}.

Following the usual calculations, the first summand yields the following
generalizations of the d’Alembert formula

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[f(x-ct)+f(x+ct)\right]+\frac{1}{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct}g(x^{\prime})\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}^{\prime}+

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\lim_{h\to+\infty}\left\{\phantom{\int_{1}^{t}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\right.\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(tS(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)\cos(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right)(x,t)-

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\left.\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\cos(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}s\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)S(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right)(x,t)\right\}

(9.25)

for all the interior points
x\in\mathring{K}
and all
t\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.
Note explicitly that (
9.25
) does not yield a uniqueness
result because
\Delta_{u}
depends on
u\left(\pm k,-\right)
and

\partial_{x}u\left(\pm k,-\right)
(see (
9.20
)). This
proves the following

Theorem 82
.

Let
f
,
g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right])

and assume that
u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right]\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})

is a solution of the wave equation (
9.18
) subject to the
initial conditions (
9.19
). Then necessarily
u(x,t)

satisfies relation (
9.25
) at all interior points
x\in\mathring{K}

and all
t\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. In particular, if we also assume
that
u\left(\pm k,-\right)=0=\partial_{x}u\left(\pm k,-\right)
,
we get the usual d’Alembert solution, and if in addition we take
f=0
,

g=\delta
, we get the wave kernel
u(x,t)=\frac{1}{2c}\left[H(x+ct)-H(x-ct)\right]
.

Now, we want to see how to revert the previous steps to obtain a sufficient
condition. Given GSF
F_{+}
,
F_{-}
,
G_{+}
,
G_{-}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
,
set for all
\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
and all
t\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

\Delta_{\pm}^{k}(\omega,t):=i\omega\left(F_{+}(t)e^{-ik\cdot\omega}-F_{-}(t)e^{ik\cdot\omega}\right)+\left(G_{+}(t)e^{-ik\cdot\omega}-G_{-}(t)e^{ik\cdot\omega}\right)

(9.26)

Let
W_{\pm}^{k}(x,t)
be the function defined by the right hand
side of (
9.25
) with
\Delta_{\pm}^{k}
instead of
\Delta_{u}
,
i.e. for all
(x,t)\in K\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle W_{\pm}^{k}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[f(x-ct)+f(x+ct)\right]+\frac{1}{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct}g(x^{\prime})\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}^{\prime}+

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\lim_{h\to+\infty}\left\{\phantom{\int_{1}^{t}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\right.\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(tS(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}\Delta_{\pm}^{k}(\omega,s)\cos(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right)(x,t)-

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\left.\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\cos(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}s\Delta_{\pm}^{k}(\omega,s)S(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right)(x,t)\right\}

(9.27)

(we are clearly implicitly assuming that such limit exists, which
is, on the other hand a necessary condition of the previous deduction).
We assume the compatibility conditions

\displaystyle F_{+}(t)

\displaystyle=W_{\pm}^{k}(k,t),\quad F_{-}(t)=W_{\pm}^{k}(-k,t)

\displaystyle G_{+}(t)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}W_{\pm}^{k}(k,t),\quad G_{-}(t)=\partial_{x}W_{\pm}^{k}(-k,t)

(9.28)

(as usual, we will see that they are redundant because having a solution
of the DE imply further restrictions on these functions). Finally,
set
u(x,t):=W_{\pm}^{k}(x,t)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
for all
(x,t)\in K\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.
Conditions (
9.28
) and (
9.26
) imply

\Delta_{\pm}^{k}(\omega,t)=i\omega\Delta_{1k}\left(u(-,t)\right)(\omega)+\Delta_{1k}\left(\partial_{x}u(-,t)\right)(\omega),

(9.29)

which is an important equality to reverse all the previous steps.
In fact, applying
\mathcal{F}_{k}
to both side of the equality

u(x,t)=W_{\pm}^{k}(x,t)
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)(\omega,t)
