\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[f(x-ct)+f(x+ct)\right]+\frac{1}{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct}g(x^{\prime})\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}^{\prime}\right)(\omega,t)+

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\lim_{h\to+\infty}\left\{\phantom{\int_{1}^{t}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\right.\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(tS(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)\cos(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right)\right.-

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\left.\left.\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\cos(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}s\Delta_{u}(\omega,s)S(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right)(x,t)\right\}\right)(\omega,t).

(9.30)

The first summand can be written as

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[f(x-ct)+f(x+ct)\right]+\frac{1}{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct}g(x^{\prime})\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}^{\prime}\right)(\omega,t)=\\
=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(d_{2}(\omega)tS(c\omega t)+d_{1}(\omega)\cos(c\omega t)\right)\right)(\omega,t).

As above, we can exchange
\mathcal{F}_{k}
and
\lim_{h\to+\infty}

because of Thm.
35
. Thereby, applying the Fourier
inversion Thm.
61
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}(u)(\omega,t)

\displaystyle=d_{2}(\omega)tS(c\omega t)+d_{1}(\omega)\cos(c\omega t)+

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+tS(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}\Delta_{\pm}^{k}(\omega,s)\cos(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}-

(9.31)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\cos(c\omega t)\int_{1}^{t}s\Delta_{\pm}^{k}(\omega,s)S(c\omega s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}},

which holds for all interior point
\omega\in K
and for all
t\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Reversing the previous calculations, we arrive at

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)}{\partial t^{2}}(\omega,t)+c^{2}\omega^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)(\omega,t)=\Delta_{\pm}^{k}(\omega,t).

Now, we can substitute (
9.29
) and use the derivation
formula Thm.
57
.
(viii)
, to get

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial t^{2}}\right)(\omega,t)=c^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}}\right)(\omega,t)\qquad\forall\omega\in\mathring{K}\,\forall t\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}.

We finally assume the extensibility property for the wave equation:
If the HFT of the wave equation holds on
\mathring{K}\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
,
then it also holds on
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. This allows
us to apply
\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}
to both sides and use again the
Fourier inversion theorem:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial t^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=c^{2}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}}\right)\right)\qquad\forall h\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial t^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle(x,t)=c^{2}\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}}\right)\right)(x,t),

\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial t^{2}}(x,t)=c^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}}(x,t)

(9.32)

for all
x\in\mathring{K}
and all
t
, and hence also for all
x\in K

by continuity. It is important to note that equation (
9.32
)
implies the usual compatibility conditions (see e.g.
[
36
]

for similar calculations):

\displaystyle F_{+}(0)=f(h),\quad F^{\prime}_{+}(0)=g(h),\quad F^{\prime\prime}_{+}(0)=c^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(h)

\displaystyle F_{-}(0)=f(-h),\quad F^{\prime}_{-}(0)=g(-h),\quad F^{\prime\prime}_{-}(0)=c^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(-h)

\displaystyle g(k-ct)-f^{\prime}(k-ct)=F^{\prime}_{+}(t)-G_{+}(t)

\displaystyle g(-k-ct)+f^{\prime}(-k-ct)=F^{\prime}_{+}(t)+G_{+}(t)

Therefore, conditions (
9.28
) over-determine the
functions
F_{\pm}
and
G_{\pm}
which hence cannot be freely chosen.
In particular, if
f
,
g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
, and we take
F_{\pm}=G_{\pm}=0
,
then
u(\pm k,-)=0=\partial_{x}u(\pm k,-)
for some
k\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

sufficiently large, and hence the compatibility conditions (
9.19
)
always hold.

This proves the following

Theorem 83
.

Let
f
,
g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right])
,

F_{+}
,
F_{-}
,
G_{+}
,
G_{-}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
.
Define
\Delta_{\pm}^{k}
as in (
9.26
),
W_{\pm}^{k}

as in (
9.27
) (assuming that the corresponding
\lim_{h\to+\infty}

exists) and
u(x,t):=W_{\pm}^{k}(x,t)
for all
(x,t)\in K\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.
Assume the compatibility conditions (
9.28
), and
the extensibility property: If
\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial t^{2}}\right)=c^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}}\right)

holds in
\mathring{K}\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, then it also holds
on
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. Then
u
satisfies the wave
equation on
K\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. In particular, if
F_{\pm}=G_{\pm}=0
,
then
u
also satisfies the initial conditions (
9.19
).
Finally, if
f
,
g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
, then the compatibility
conditions (
9.19
) always hold for some
k\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

sufficiently large, and
u
is given by the usual d’Alembert formula.

Explicitly note that even the last case,
F_{\pm}=G_{\pm}=0

and
f
,
g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
, includes for
f
and
g
a large
class of GSF, e.g. non-linear operations
F\left(\left(\delta^{(p)}\right)^{a}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<a\leq A\\
0\leq p\leq P\end{subarray}}
of Dirac delta and its derivatives, such that
F\left(0\right)=0
.

The Heat equation

Let us consider the one dimensional (generalized) heat equation

\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}=a^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}},\quad u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right]\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}),

(9.33)

(where
a\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
,
t\leq-\frac{N}{a^{2}k^{2}}\log\left(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\right)
,

N\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
) and subject to the initial conditions at
t=0

u(-,0)=f,

(9.34)

where
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right])
. Applying, as usual, the HFT
with respect to the variable
x
to both sides of (
9.33
)
and Thm.
57
.
(viii)
we get

\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)}{\partial t}=-a^{2}\omega^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)+i\omega\Delta_{1k}u+\Delta_{1k}\left(\partial_{x}u\right).

For all
\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, set

\displaystyle\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right)

\displaystyle:=i\omega\Delta_{1k}\left(u(-,t)\right)+\Delta_{1k}\left(\partial_{x}u(-,t)\right).

Therefore, we get

\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)}{\partial t}\left(\omega,-\right)+a^{2}\omega^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)\left(\omega,-\right)=\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,-\right)\quad\forall\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}.

(9.35)

Solving (
9.35
) with the integrating factor
\mu\left(t\right):=e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}\intop_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}}=e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}t}

(which is well-defined if
\omega\in K
because we assumed that
t\leq-\frac{N}{a^{2}k^{2}}\log\left(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\right)
),
we have

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)\left(\omega,t\right)=\frac{\intop_{0}^{t}e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}+c(\omega)}{e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}t}},

where
c(\omega):=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)\left(\omega,0\right)=\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
,
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)\left(\omega,t\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\intop_{0}^{t}e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}+\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)e^{-a^{2}\omega^{2}t}

\displaystyle=e^{-a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\intop_{0}^{t}e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}+\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)\mathcal{F}\left(\frac{1}{2a\sqrt{\pi t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4a^{2}t}}\right)(\omega,t)

\displaystyle=:e^{-a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\intop_{0}^{t}e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}+\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)\mathcal{F}\left(H_{t}^{a}(x)\right)(\omega,t)

\displaystyle=e^{-a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\intop_{0}^{t}e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}+\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f*H_{t}^{a}\right)(\omega,t),

where
H_{t}^{a}(x):=\frac{1}{2a\sqrt{\pi t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4a^{2}t}}

is the heat kernel (which, in our setting, is a compactly supported
GSF). Finally, applying the inversion Thm.
61
and the convolution
formula Thm.
57
.
(x)
we get

u(x,t)=(f*H_{t}^{a})(x,t)+\mathcal{F}_{k}^{-1}\left(e^{-a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\intop_{0}^{t}e^{a^{2}\omega^{2}t}\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right)\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}\right)(x,t).

(9.36)

As usual, if
\Delta_{u}\left(\omega,t\right)
equals zero, we obtain
the classical solution. This proves the following

Theorem 84
.

Let
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right])
, and assume
that
u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right]\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
is a solution
of the heat equation (
9.33
), where
a\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
,
t\leq-\frac{N}{a^{2}k^{2}}\log\left(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\right)
,

N\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, subject to initial condition (
9.34
).
Then necessarily
u(x,t)
satisfies (
9.36
) at all interior
points
x\in\mathring{K}
and all
t\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. In particular,
if we also assume that
u(\pm k,-)=0=\partial_{x}u(\pm k,-)
, we
get the usual solution, and if in addition we take
f=\delta
, then
we get the heat kernel
u(x,t)=H_{t}^{a}(x)=\frac{1}{2a\sqrt{\pi t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4a^{2}t}}
.

It is now clear how one can proceed to obtain a sufficient condition
for the heat equation similar to Thm.
83
, and for
this reason, we omit it here.

Laplace’s equation

Actually, we show this example only for the sake of completeness,
but we present here only a preliminary study. Let us consider the
one dimensional Laplace’s equation

\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial y^{2}}=0\ ,u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}([-k,k]\times[0,-\frac{N}{k}\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}]),

(9.37)

where
N\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, and subject to the boundary conditions at
y=0

\displaystyle u(-,0)

\displaystyle=f,\quad\partial_{y}u(-,0)=0,

(9.38)

\displaystyle u(\pm k,-)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\partial_{x}u(\pm k,-)=0,

(9.39)

where
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\left[-k,k\right])
. Set
Y:=[0,-\frac{N}{k}\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}]\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
By applying the HFT with respect to
x
and Thm.
57
.
(viii)
,
the problem is converted into

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)}{\partial y^{2}}=\omega^{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)

(9.40)

because of (
9.39
). The general solution of (
9.40
)
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}(u)(\omega,y)

\displaystyle=d_{1}(\omega)e^{\omega y}+d_{2}(\omega)e^{-\omega y},

where the functions
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
satisfy
\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)=d_{1}(\omega)+d_{2}(\omega)

and
\partial_{y}\mathcal{F}_{k}(u)(\omega,0)=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\partial_{y}u(-,0)\right)(\omega)=0=\omega d_{1}(\omega)-\omega d_{2}(\omega)

because
\partial_{y}u(-,0)=0
. Since the set of invertible numbers
in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
is dense in the sharp topology, we hence have

d_{1}(\omega)=d_{2}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega).

Note that
e^{\pm\omega y}
is well defined for all
\omega\in K

and all
y\in Y=[0,-\frac{N}{k}\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}]
. Finally, applying
the inversion Thm.
61
, we get

\displaystyle u(x,y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)(x,y)

(9.41)

for all
(x,y)\in\mathring{K}\times Y
. Note that a term of the type

\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\cosh(\omega y)\right)
cannot be considered
if
h\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
is sufficiently large and
y
is invertible because

\cosh(\pm hy)
would yield a non
\rho
-moderate number. Thereby,
we cannot transform the product in (
9.41
) into a
convolution.

Theorem 85
.

Let
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, and set
Y:=[0,-\frac{N}{k}\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}]
.
Assume that
u\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K\times Y)
is a solution of the Laplace’s
equation subject to the boundary conditions (
9.38
).
Then necessarily
u(x,y)
satisfies relation (
9.41
)
for all
(x,y)\in\mathring{K}\times Y
. In particular, if
f=\delta

then instead of
\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)

in (
9.41
) we can take
\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathbb{1}\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)
.

It is well-known that if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
is
a classical smooth function, then
f
is necessarily an analytic
function (see
[
33
]
and e.g.
[
34
]
). Assuming
that the classical Hadamard result
[
33
]
can be extended to
GSF, this would not exclude the case
f=\delta
, which can be proved
to be an analytic GSF.

To reverse the previous steps, assume that

\displaystyle f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(H),\quad H\Subset_{\text{\rm f}}\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\exists C,B\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\,\forall x\in H\,\forall j\in\mathbb{N}:\ \left|f^{(j)}(x)\right|\leq C\cdot B^{j}.

(9.42)

Note that if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
is an ordinary smooth function
and
C
,
B\in\mathbb{R}
, assumption (
9.42
) implies
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\omega}(H\cap\mathbb{R})
,
i.e.
f
is real analytic. Moreover, for the sake of clarity, finally
note that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\omega}(H\cap\mathbb{R})\cap\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(H)
implies
only
f(x_{\varepsilon})\sim_{\sigma}0
for all
[x_{\varepsilon}]\notin H
, but
it does not imply
f=0
.

The previous assumptions and the Riemann-Lebesgue Lem.
52

yield that also
\mathcal{F}(f)
is compactly supported in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Define

u(x,y):=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)(x,y)\qquad\forall x\in K\,\forall y\in Y.

(9.43)

Since
\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)
is compactly supported
in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
and satisfies the assumptions of Riemann-Lebesgue
Lem.
52
, we have that also
u(-,y)
is compactly
supported in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. We hence assume to have considered
k

sufficiently large so that

u(\pm k,-)=0=\partial_{x}u(\pm k,-).

(9.44)

We now proceed in the usual way:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(u\right)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)\right)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)\right)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)\right)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(f)(\omega)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)

(9.45)

for all
\omega\in\mathring{K}
and all
y\in Y
. Since
i\omega\Delta_{1k}\left(u(-,y)\right)+\Delta_{1k}\left(\partial_{x}u(-,y)\right)=0
,
from (
9.45
) we can revert the previous calculations
to obtain

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x^{2}}\right)(\omega,y)+\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial y^{2}}\right)(\omega,y)=0\qquad\forall(\omega,y)\in\mathring{K}\times Y.

(9.46)

Once again, we assume that our solution
u
satisfies the extensibility
property, i.e. that (
9.46
) implies that the same
equation holds on
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\times Y
. A final application of the
Fourier inversion Thm.
61
yields that
u
satisfies
(
9.37
). Setting
y=0
in (
9.43
) we
obtain the first boundary condition in (
9.38
). Finally,

\partial_{y}\left(\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)\right)=\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\omega\sinh(\omega y)\right)

converges for
h\to+\infty
for all fixed
y\in Y
because
\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\omega\sinh(\omega y)

is compactly supported (see (
3.3
)). Since
Y

is functionally compact, Thm.
35
implies that
the convergence of these partial derivatives is actually uniform on

Y
. Thereby
\partial_{y}u(x,0)=\left.\lim_{h\to+\infty}\partial_{y}\left(\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)\right)\right|_{y=0}=0
.

Theorem 86
.

Assume that
f
satisfies (
9.42
) and that for all
x\in K
,

y\in Y:=[0,-\frac{N}{k}\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}]

\exists\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}(f)\cdot\cosh(\omega y)\right)(x,y)=:u(x,y).

Finally, assume that
u
satisfies the extensibility property on

\mathring{K}\times Y
for the Laplace’s equation and
k
is sufficiently
large so that (
9.44
) holds. Then
u
satisfies the
Laplace’s equations (
9.37
) and the boundary conditions
(
9.38
) and (
9.39
).

9.3.
Applications to convolution equations.

Consider the following convolution equation in
y

g=f*y,

(9.47)

where we assume that
y
,
g\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)
and
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
.
As in the classical theory, we apply the convolution Thm.
57
.
(x)

to get

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(g\right)=\mathcal{F}\left(f\right)\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(y\right).

Assuming that
\mathcal{F}\left(f\right)(\omega)
is invertible for
all
\omega\in K
, the inversion Thm.
61
yields

y(t)=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(g\right)}{\mathcal{F}\left(f\right)}\right)(t),\quad\forall t\in\mathring{K}.

For example, to highlight the differences with the classical
theory, let us consider the convolution equation
(\delta^{\prime}+\delta)*y=\delta

with
y(-1)=0
. We have
g=\delta
, and
f=\delta^{\prime}+\delta
so that

\mathcal{F}(f)=i\omega\mathbb{1}+\mathbb{1}
, where as usual
\mathbb{1}=\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(\delta\right)
.
Since
\mathbb{1}(\omega)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, the quantity
i\omega\mathbb{1}(\omega)+\mathbb{1}(\omega)

is always invertible, and hence we obtain

y(t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{\mathbb{1}}{i\omega\mathbb{1}+\mathbb{1}}\right)(t),\quad\forall t\in\mathring{K}.

It is easy to prove that
y(t)+y^{\prime}(t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(1|_{K}\right)\left(t\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-k}^{k}e^{i\omega t}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}=\frac{k}{\pi}S(kt)

(see (
9.24
)) and hence
y(t)=e^{-t}\frac{k}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{t}S(kx)e^{x}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}

e.g. for all
\log(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}})\leq t\leq-\log(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}})
. Therefore

y(t)=e^{-t}\int_{-1}^{t}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(1|_{K}\right)(s)e^{s}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\approx e^{-t}\int_{-1}^{t}\delta(s)e^{s}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}=e^{-t}H(t),

for all
t\in\mathring{K}
which are far from the origin, i.e. such
that
|t|\geq r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for some
r
.

10.
Conclusions

In the introduction of this article, we motivated the natural attempts
of several authors to extend the domain of some kind of Fourier transform.
The HFT presented in this paper can be applied to the entire space
of all the GSF defined in the infinite interval
[-k,k]^{n}
. These
clearly include all tempered Schwartz distributions, all tempered
Colombeau GF, but also a large class of non-tempered GF, such as the
exponential functions, or non-linear examples like
\delta^{a}\circ\delta^{b}
,

\delta^{a}\circ H^{b}
,
a
,
b\in\mathbb{N}
, etc.

We want to close by listing some features of the theory that allow
some of the main results we saw:

(i)

The power of a non-Archimedean language permeates the whole theory
since the beginning (e.g. by defining GF as set-theoretical maps
with infinite values derivatives or in the use of sharp continuity).
This power turned out to be important also for the HFT: see the heuristic
motivation of the FT in Sec.
4.1
,
Example
70
about application of the uncertainty
principle to a delta distribution, or the HFT of exponential functions
in Example
55
and in Sec.
9
.

(ii)

The results presented here are deeply founded on a strong and flexible
theory of multidimensional integration of GSF on functionally compact
sets: the possibility to exchange hyperlimits and integration has
been used several times in the present work; the possibility to compute

\varepsilon
-wise integrals on intervals is another feature used in several
theorems and a key step in defining integration of compactly supported
GSF.

(iii)

It can also be worth explicitly mentioning that the definition of
HFT is based on the classical formulas used only for rapidly decreasing
smooth functions and not on duality pairing. In our opinion, this
is a strong simplification that even underscores more the strict analogies
between ordinary smooth functions and GSF. All this in spite of the
fact that the ring of scalars
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
is not a field and is not totally
ordered.

(iv)

Important differences with respect to the classical theory result
from the Riemann-Lebesgue Lem.
52
and the
differentiation formula (
6.1
). In the former case,
we explained these differences as a general consequence of integration
by part formula, i.e. of the non-linear framework we are working
in, see Thm.
54
. The compact support of the HFT
\mathbb{1}

of Dirac’s delta reveals to be very important in stating and proving
the preservation properties of HFT, see Sec.
8
.
Surprisingly (the classical formula dates back at least to 1822),
in Sec.
9
we showed that the new
differentiation formula is very important to get out of the constrained
world of tempered solutions.

(v)

Finally, Example
70
of application of the
uncertainty principle, further suggests that the space
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K)

may be a useful framework for quantum mechanics, so as to have both
GF and smooth functions in a space sharing several properties with
the classical
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(but which, on the other hand, is a

graded
Hilbert space).

Acknowledgement.
We are grateful to Michael Oberguggenberger
and Sanja Konjik for their careful reading of our work. In particular,
for the stimulating discussions with Michael, which considerably improved
this manuscript.
